My next board matter concerns the Veterans and Military Families Commission survey results, and I want to acknowledge I can see you're way up there, all the way up, almost in the back row, former Supervisor Penny Gross, who is here with us, who plays a vital role in this commission. I want to thank you for your service. Last November, the Fairfax County Veterans

[01:08:24] and Military Families Commission presented its inaugural report to the board and announced that a survey of veterans and military families would commence in January. The survey was open from January 15th to February 24th, 2026, and distributed through a variety of online and social media platforms focused on veterans and the military. The survey results have been analyzed by the commission and county staff and are ready to be shared with the board and neighboring jurisdictions. Areas measured were housing, food security, education, childcare, employment, and mental health substance abuse. High costs were the top

[01:09:02] concerns for housing, childcare, and food security. Seventy-three percent of respondents looking to rent or own a home in Fairfax cited rent and mortgage costs as their primary challenge. Respondents reported below 50 percent satisfaction on most education questions. Fewer than 50% of respondents were familiar with most services and support opportunities shared throughout the survey. 15% of respondents indicated they sometimes or often couldn't afford to eat balanced meals. Only 5% of respondents reported having struggled with substance abuse or addiction at some point. The Commission has identified housing along with education and childcare as priorities to guide its

[01:09:41] work during the next year. The cost burden for housing both rental and ownership was the strongest finding of the survey as housing stability affects every other issue. A large percentage of respondents were unaware of any of the county's affordable housing assistance programs. Improving access to and visibility of existing housing programs and resources for veterans and military families can be assisted by including information on the Veterans and Military Families webpage. For military families, as with non-military families, Child care is directly connected to spouse employment, family income, food security, and quality of life.

[01:10:19] Child care access on military installations often is over-enrolled, so parents seek alternatives off-post. The commission plans to have discussions with local military commanders to figure out what can be done between the county and the installation to help this shared issue, including regulatory hurdles that may impede establishment of new child care facilities.

[01:10:40] Although many of the survey respondents did not have children enrolled in Fairfax County public schools, those who did reported below 50 percent satisfaction across most education metrics, including military-connected resources and special education.

[01:10:57] Military families experience frequent transitions and weak transition support for students. Additionally, inconsistent counseling and special education gaps can lead to military-connected children falling behind their non-military peers. FCPS is working to achieve Purple Star status for all its schools within the next school year, but more attention should focus on gaps in documentation and special education IEP support.

[01:11:23] The Commission is not treating these items as separate issues. The survey pointed to a connected stability problem, high cost of living, child care barriers, school transition concerns, service navigation gaps, and mental health access challenges. The commission's new web page on the county website can identify resources and expand the commission's ability to bring issues to county agency partners and to the board.

[01:11:47] To highlight the long-awaited survey results and alert our veterans and military community to county resources, I ask without objection that the Office of Public Affairs be directed to prepare an appropriate news release and information for distribution, especially to veterans' organizations, newsletters, and military-connected installations in Fairfax County.

[01:12:07] And I so move. Without objection, it's so ordered. And let me just add, as I mentioned, thanking Supervisor Gross, I want to thank all the members of the commission. And, you know, having grown up literally at Fort Belvoir and, you know, representing that area for a long time, I know I speak for a lot of people on this dais that the needs of the military community, while many of them are the same in here, there are also very distinct characteristics that we need to be mindful of.

[01:12:38] And seeing some of these survey results, especially the food insecurity one, is troubling. I mean, these are our national heroes, active service members and veterans who have served our community for a long time that are struggling, and we need to make sure we acknowledge that and do the proper reach out.

[01:12:57] And then finally, I do want to comment on the school piece for a moment, because as I visit some of the schools with high concentrations of military-connected children, the concerns that are raised in this survey are the same concerns that people working in those schools with military families have, which is, you know, there's not, you know, a lot of advanced planning that goes in.

[01:13:20] Kids just arrive. They get transferred. They get moved from one part of the country, come into Virginia, into FCPS. And not only are they dealing with the challenges within their own family and learning to acclimate to a new community, they may have had a very different academic background than other kids that are already in their classroom.

[01:13:41] And that doesn't get acknowledged enough. And I want to thank particularly the existing counselors in our FCPS schools that deal with this issue all the time. I will never forget talking to one of them at Woodlawn Elementary School, which is right near Fort Belvoir, And she told me that the most frustrating part for her is spending hundreds and hundreds of hours working with children that need her direct support.

[01:14:06] And then one day, that child is just gone, transferred somewhere else in the country. And she's starting over when a new child comes in. And just to reflect on that work and what those people face on a day-to-day basis, I think, is important for all of us to acknowledge.

[01:14:27] And so I look forward to the continued work of the commission, and most importantly, the continued support of our county agencies with our military and veterans-connected families. [01:15:50] And these were people who live in our community that have roots in Venezuela, have families still in Venezuela, who immigrated to the United States and just wanted to come out and say thank you to our FRD, to the USAR. On December 9, 2025, the board adopted phase one of the Comprehensive Plan Policy Plan Amendment, known as Plan Forward, which updated the land use, transportation, environment, and parks and recreation elements and added a new healthy communities element.

[01:19:19] The updated land use element introduced the concept of suburban village centers, which are mixed-use community focal points within suburban neighborhoods. These transit-ready areas along arterial roadways provide a range of services adjacent to lower-density communities that may be suitable for mixed-use development, including medium-intensity residential use.

[01:19:42] The Board has advanced similar efforts for sites such as the Pan Am Shopping Center, Graham Park Shopping Center, and more recently, Franconia Triangle, as separate plan amendments. However, many other commercial and civic nodes have not been reconsidered since our original planning decades ago.

[01:20:00] At the same time, the Housing Action Plan prioritizes expanding the housing opportunities countywide. While much of the comprehensive plan focuses on new growth in our mixed-use centers, the suburban neighborhoods constitute a significant portion of the county's land area, and establishing a new planning framework for appropriate sites in these areas is an essential step to expanding housing opportunities.

[01:20:24] Staff have begun identifying potential locations that may merit consideration for suburban village-centered designation based on criteria established in the land-use element, and it is appropriate to allow this work to move forward. These areas vary widely in their characteristics, community needs, and goals, so a one-size-fits-all approach will not be feasible.

[01:20:46] The final list of criteria and suburban village-centered designations is expected to reflect this diversity. [01:26:23] Furthermore, he supported the ship's mission when it deployed to the newly liberated city of Manila. Tucked along the Potomac River, Dyke March is a remarkable freshwater tidal wetland home to hundreds of species of plants and wildlife and offers residents a place to experience nature right in our backyard.

[01:53:44] For 50 years, the Friends of Dyke March, or FODM, have worked to make sure this special place is protected for generations to come. They were founded in 1976 by a group of really active and committed individuals who wanted to make sure that the preservation and the restoration of this marsh took place.

[01:54:07] Their work reaches far beyond the marsh itself from scientific studies, bird surveys, and water quality monitoring to nature walks, invasive plant removal, trash cleanups, and educational programs. The Friends have also been a crucial voice and advocate in efforts to restore and stabilize the marsh in partnership with the National Park Service.

[01:54:28] So, again, year after year, they clearly have continued to care. Glenda Booth has been a crucial part of that, not only as the current president and many-time president of FODM, but also a tireless community leader who puts so much of her time and energy into the FODM.

[01:54:43] And I would say especially she writes the Marsh Wren, which, if you don't get it, is a great newsletter talking about the park, the Dyke Marsh, and then what all that it means. And it's a great way to learn more about how that is being restored over time.

[01:55:00] Again, for 50 years, the Friends of Dyke Marsh have reminded us that protecting our parks, trails, waterways, and natural areas takes people willing to speak up. That motion carries. Supervisor Herity. The next one is a board matter jointly with Supervisors Alcorn and Luss to have staff review treatment of glass as a recyclable material.

[02:13:45] In 2019, collecting glass in single-stream curbside recycling bins was a major problem for several reasons. There was no market for glass, so it was being landfilled. It was contaminating the other recyclable materials, and the resulting glass dust was a problem for some recycling equipment.

[02:14:05] As a result, glass was being landfilled and not recycled. A program called the Purple Can Club was founded to allow residents the opportunity to recycle glass in a roll-off dumpsters around the county. County staff were successful in developing several markets for glass collected in the Purple Cans, including use in road beds.

[02:14:24] The Purple Can has been extremely popular, but residents regularly express a desire to return to a more convenient single-stream curbside glass collection. I was impressed with how far recycling technology has come during my tour this summer of Waste Management's new State of the Article Materials Recovery Facility in Elkridge, Maryland, this summer.

[02:14:46] Not only have they increased the speed and quality of the process, but they have reduced the processing cost. The facility can process 685 tons of recycling material in a single day using two lines. Glass is pulled out of the front of the process and no longer presents a challenge to the machinery or other recyclables.

[02:15:07] While in Elkridge, I went next door to visit Cap Glass's transfer facility. Cap Glass is the company that actually recycles the glass from both the Waste Management Facility and Fairfax County's purple can bins. Vastly different from 2019, the glass brought here is not going to landfills, but is being recycled in everything from beer and wine bottles to mason jars after being sorted by color.

[02:15:32] The glass is not able to be used for those purposes. It's crushed to make sand for high-quality filters because it is processed. The sand has many advantages over actual sand. County staff, the board, and I are proud of what the Purple Can Club has accomplished in the last seven years, but it may be time to return to single-stream curbside recycling.

[02:15:55] Therefore, Mr. Chairman, I move the Board of Directors, direct staff, to evaluate the county's current guidance on glass as a recyclable material and the potential efficiencies associated with phasing out or otherwise involving the Purple Can Club. This should include an evaluation of the current cost to the Fairfax County associated with providing the service, potential savings, or other possible efficiencies, and associated guidance to Fairfax County residents on recycled glass.

[04:49:02] Fairfax plan which directed staff to update the public facilities manual to incorporate climate projections and enhance resilience to improve tree canopy health and to reduce tree losses by encouraging developers and project arborist to select tree species that are more resilient to a range of climatic hazards and urban environmental conditions.

[04:49:30] Staff is also directed to incorporate climate projections and to integrate climate considerations into tree selection. The key proposed changes in this amendment include updating the tree selection and canopy cover guide table in Chapter 12 and increases the number of native trees to Fairfax County and to surrounding areas to accommodate the shifting hardiness zones.

[04:49:55] It expands the trait information for trees to include additional climate adaptation and resilience strengths and vulnerabilities. Overall, these proposed amendments align the public facilities manual with the zoning ordinance and include tree species with the highest climate resiliency with the greatest likelihood of long-term survivability in a changing climate.

[06:08:42] And next, we have a public hearing to consider amendments to Chapter 122, Tree Conservation Ordinance, Article 9, Tree Commission of the Code of the County of Fairfax, Virginia. As soon as staff is ready, we would welcome a staff presentation.

[06:09:21] Thank you, Mr. Chairman. My name is Brianne Fuller with the Office of the County Executive, and with me today are staff from the Department of Public Works and Environmental Services and the Office of Environmental and Energy Coordination. The proposed county code amendment is part of a broader review of the county's boards, authorities, and commissions, or BACs, that began with a board matter in 2024.

[06:09:42] This particular recommendation focuses on the county's environmental advisory bodies, specifically the tree commission and the Environmental Quality Advisory Council, or EQUAC. This proposes to bring them together into one overarching environmental commission. This recommendation spans two of today's items, the county code amendment to dissolve the tree commission and the consideration item to adopt bylaws that restructure EQAC as part of establishing this overarching environmental commission, which would include the tree commission's purview.

[06:10:12] The goals are to reduce scope overlap, use staff resources more efficiently, and continue to modernize our processes, while strengthening the role of trees and natural resources within the broader context of the environmental advisory framework.

[06:10:27] The county's environmental work has expanded significantly since both the Tree Commission and EQUAC were formed more than 50 years ago each. Today, the county has significantly greater staff capacity and expertise, including a division within the Department of Public Works and Environmental Services dedicated to urban and community forestry, and an entire department dedicated to environmental policy and programming, the Office of Environmental and Energy Coordination.

[06:10:54] Many environmental programs and policies have been implemented over the years as well. Just a few examples of those include the Tree Action Plan, the Tree Conservation Ordinance, and Resilient Fairfax. Community involvement has expanded as well, including the creation of the Tree Community of Practice.

[06:11:11] This recommendation builds on the county's strong commitment to trees and the environment by creating a coordinated advisory structure that brings together related environmental issues and expertise. It would create a more streamlined advisory process and focuses staff support on one coordinated body addressing these interconnected issues.

[06:11:30] The proposed structure maintains dedicated representation for trees and natural resources with two seats for residents that would have this expertise. In addition, trees and forestry are a mandated focus area for each annual work plan that the commission would create.

[06:11:47] The proposed bylaws also include several changes intended to modernize the commission's structure and operations, including more concise reporting, an annual work plan, a flexible scope that can respond to changes over time, and the expansion of student membership from one to two members, which builds on the success that EQUAC has seen with their student membership. Following the recommendation to the Board of Supervisors, I met with the Tree Commission and EQUAC in March and April to hear their initial feedback on the recommendation. I then met with both groups again in June to discuss a proposed commission structure and receive feedback on that structure prior to finalizing draft bylaws.

[06:12:23] Those draft bylaws were then disseminated for their review and comment, and their comments were utilized when finalizing the bylaws that you see today. Responses to the groups were also provided earlier this month. In closing, this recommendation builds on the county's long-standing commitment to trees and environmental issues by evolving the advisory structure alongside the county's environmental priorities. This brings related expertise together while maintaining a strong focus on trees and natural resources. That concludes my presentation, and we are happy to answer questions. Thank you. Thank you very much for that presentation. At this time, are there any questions for staff? Seeing and hearing none, this is a public hearing. I do have

[06:13:03] a number of speakers who have signed up in advance to testify on this matter. If you are here to testify on this matter, please be sure you're down close and ready to testify. Speaker number one is Lynn Molston, to be followed by Jennifer Falcone, if I called your name. Please be ready to testify. Welcome. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Supervisor Alcorn, members of the board. My name is Lynn Molston. I serve as president of Reston Citizens Association. I apologize for overseeing the introduction earlier. Thank you for the opportunity to speak. I urge you not to dissolve

[06:13:40] the Tree Commission and the Environmental Quality Advisory Council, or EQUAC, and replace them with a single environmental commission. For more than 50 years, these bodies have provided exactly the kind of informed, independent, focused advice that advisory groups are meant to offer. EQUAC has served as Fairfax County's environmental watchdog, producing annual reports on the environment and advising the board on air and water quality, land use, energy, and climate policy. For 53 years, the Tree Commission has brought specialized practical expertise

[06:14:14] on urban forestry, tree preservation, and the Tree Conservation Ordinance. That experience, institutional knowledge, and community trust should not be casually merged away. Staff have said consolidation could save approximately 600 staff hours and $20,000 in meeting planning costs. Efficiency matters, but these advisory groups help inform decisions involving billions of dollars in land use, infrastructure, stormwater, and climate investments. Saving $20,000 while

[06:14:50] reducing independent oversight would be penny-wise and pound-foolish. I have three concerns. First, Trees will lose focused advocacy at precisely the wrong time. Across Fairfax County, residents are watching mature trees disappear as infill development and redevelopment accelerate.

[06:15:09] They are confused and frustrated by how the Tree Conservation Ordinances applies and what protections actually exist. The appropriate response is to strengthen the Tree Commission's ability to advise the board and educate the public, not dissolve it.

[06:15:25] Under the proposed Environmental Commission, trees would become one issue among many, with only one seat designated for forestry or natural resources. Although I heard that you all have upped that to two. Bravo. Today, the Tree Commission includes multiple members with specialized knowledge of urban forestry and the county's tree rules.

[06:15:47] Replacing that body with one or two tree people in the room is not simply consolidation. It is a clear reduction in expertise and attention. That is difficult to justify when the county recognizes trees as essential to resilience, stormwater management, heat mitigation, neighborhood character, and quality of life.

[06:16:09] Fairfax County has substantial tree canopy and a goal of reaching 60% canopy coverage by 2030. If that goal matters, dedicated tree advocacy should be strengthened, not diluted. Second, independent and visible environmental oversight will be weakened.

[06:16:27] EQUAC is not just another committee. It is a recognized independent watchdog. Its annual reports and presentations give residents, civic associations, and environmental organizations a clear public measure of how Fairfax County is performing on environmental issues.

[06:16:45] Folding EQUAC into a broader commission risks blurring the line between independent advice and agency implementation. The proposed commission's bylaws are still being developed, while it is expected to coordinate closely with agencies already responsible for carrying out county policy.

[06:17:04] Coordination can be useful, but accountability requires independence and visibility. At a time when residents want greater transparency, eliminating the body known for its watchdog role sends the wrong message. Third, public participation and trust may suffer.

[06:17:22] Long-standing volunteer advisory bodies represent years of service, expertise, and public engagement. Eliminating them is not merely an organizational change. It tells current and former commissioners and the communities they serve that their contributions are expendable.

[06:17:40] The Tree Commission's awards, educational programs, events, and neighborhood outreach are not incidental. They are how county environmental goals become community action. Those functions are not clearly protected in the current proposal. If the board believes change is necessary, there is a better path.

[06:18:00] First, pause the proposal and conduct an open review with the tree commission and EQUAC members, civic organizations, and the public. Second, if you create an environmental commission, do so in addition to, not instead of, these focused advisory bodies.

[06:18:17] Third, strengthen the connection between advisory groups and county ordinances so the board receives specialized advice when residences have the greatest concerns. Two seconds. This is about more than reorganization. It's about whether Fairfax County builds on more than 50 years of environmental stewardship and replaces proven independent expertise with something broader and less defined.

[06:18:44] Please vote no on dissolving the tree commission in EQAC and commit to a transparent, participatory, and expert-driven process before making changes. Thank you for your testimony. Our next speaker is Jennifer Falcone, to be followed by a video from Barbara Ryan and Jeffrey Parnes.

[06:19:03] If you are ready to testify, if I call your name, please be ready to testify. Welcome. Good evening, Chairman McKay and members of the board. I'm Jennifer Falcone, and I'm here on behalf of Citizens for Great Falls, where I serve as secretary.

[06:19:20] Today, I want to reaffirm the concerns our organization communicated to Chairman McKay and the board on August 3rd and request that the board refrain from adopting this drafted ordinance. What's in front of you today is the dissolution of a 15-member commission that spent 53 years focused month after month exclusively on trees, replaced by two reserved seats on a much broader environmental commission. We're pleased that staff increased that from one seat originally

[06:19:53] proposed, but it's still a steep drop in dedicated expertise, and it doesn't resolve the concerns we raised earlier this month. First, diluted attention. The commission meets monthly for two to three hours entirely on trees.

Something will get less attention, and it's likely to be trees. Second, loss of the statutory connection to Chapter 122, the Tree Conservation Ordinance.

[06:20:33] The Commission's deep working familiarity with Chapter 122 and Chapter 12 of the Public Facilities Manual supports how the county implements tree preservation during development review. Nothing in the draft bylaws requires that expertise on this new body.

[06:21:33] Once the commission is gone, who provides the scrutiny next time? That question matters well beyond this one amendment. [06:21:59] As that pressure grows, the county needs more dedicated tree expertise in these land use decisions, not less. None of this is happening in a vacuum. Fairfax lost roughly 737 acres of tree canopy between 2014 and 2018 alone.

Thank you for your time and considering these concerns. Thank you. Our next speaker is Barbara Ryan who has submitted video testimony. Good afternoon. My name is Barbara Ryan. I live in McLean, Virginia, and I am testifying today on

[06:23:31] behalf of the Fairfax County Tree Commission. I have served on the tree commission as the Drainesville District Representative since June 2023 and as its chair since January 2025. Since 1973, the Tree Commission has been the county's primary advisory body on its urban forest. For over half-century, Tree Commission volunteers have worked to protect, improve, and expand Fairfax County's urban forest. Now the county proposes to dissolve it and create a new environmental commission. The Tree Commission has the following concerns about this proposed action.

[06:24:06] First, it is likely to significantly reduce the county's focus on its tree canopy. The Tree Commission's singular focus on the urban forest has been its strength for over 50 years. The proposed Environmental Commission's significantly broader focus will leave trees as one of many environmental topics it must consider and address.

[06:24:27] Reduced attention to the county's tree canopy would be especially unfortunate now, Given the challenges the county faces in attaining the community-wide Energy and Climate Action Plan goal to expand the tree canopy to 60% with a minimum of 40% tree canopy coverage in every census block by 2030.

[06:24:48] Second, this action will eliminate the role the Tree Commission plays in monitoring how the county administers its tree ordinance. The timing of this is particularly unfortunate, given citizen angst about tree loss, resulting from residential infill development and overall confusion about the county's tree preservation rules.

[06:25:08] Third, the proposed Environmental Commission will not be required to undertake any of the tree-related activities that are contained in the Tree Commission Charter, including an explicit goal to advise the board on actions it might take to protect, improve, and expand the urban forest and a number of additional specific reporting requirements related to trees and natural resources.

[06:25:30] Fourth, this action will eliminate all the Tree Commission's important public outreach activities, which have enhanced public awareness about the value of trees, how to take care of trees, and the county's policies with respect to trees. Finally, this action will likely result in a dramatic reduction in tree expertise on the Environmental Commission compared to the Tree Commission, as it would replace the 15 knowledgeable Tree Commissioners with one Environmental Commission member with forestry and natural resources experience.

[06:26:01] Overall, the Tree Commission believes that this action is likely to result in a significant reduction in the county's focus on trees and natural resources and citizen involvement in tree preservation efforts at a time when the county faces challenges maintaining its tree canopy and can least afford it. To mitigate these concerns, the Tree Commission recommends that the proposed Environmental Commission be required to include at least one agenda item related to trees at each meeting, that the tree ordinance be amended to ensure that the Environmental Commission will replace the Tree Commission in its roles related to the ordinance, that the Environmental Commission's bylaws and charter include the Tree Commission charter requirements

[06:26:41] and outreach goals, and that at least three Environmental Commission members be required to demonstrate tree and natural resource experience or expertise. Thank you for the opportunity to testify on behalf of the Tree Commission.

In 1973, the county established the Office of the Arborists.

[06:50:23] In 1992, a group of dedicated trees and natural resources created the new Urban and Community Forestry Division, UCFD and DPWS. In 2019, the Office of Environmental and Energy Coordination, OEEC, was created, an entire new agency within the county.

[06:50:42] In 2023, the Forest Conservation Branch integrated into Land Development Services to make sure that coordinated work was being done between forestry reviews and the planning process. Citizen participation has expanded and evolved significantly over this time.

[06:50:59] DPWS stands up the Tree Community of Practice formed in 2019, which originated the Tree Action Plan. This has brought together public agencies, residents, private stakeholders to collaboratively protect the county's urban canopy. The Resilient Fairfax Community Advisory Group, managed by OEEC, is an additional way that residents may participate in the county's environmental work.
